10 Years at a Glance 20
[page 4]
Premier Explosives Limited (PEL), incorporated in 1980, has progressed from a commercial explosives 
manufacturer into a specialised high-energy materials company serving defence, space, mining and 
infrastructure requirements. The Company’s evolution has been shaped by indigenous technology, 
disciplined manufacturing practices and the ability to translate energetic-material expertise into mission-
critical products for national programmes.
The FY26 operating environment reflected both the strength and timing-linked nature of this portfolio. 
Revenue from operations stood at ₹ 3,883.4 million. Defence and Space services contributed ₹ 3,154 
million, representing 81% of annual revenue, while Commercial Explosives contributed ₹ 729 million. 
FY26 revenue was lower than FY25, primarily because of ban on electric detonators production; however, 
profitability improved substantially, supported by higher other income and a favourable mix of products.
From Energetic Materials to Strategic Readiness
A broader high-energy portfolio
PEL manufactures and supports products across solid propellants, 
pyrotechnic devices, countermeasure systems, explosive 
compositions, detonators, detonating fuse, cast boosters and bulk 
explosives. Its defence portfolio includes propellants for missile 
systems, Chaffs and Flares, pyrogen igniters, rocket motors, pyro 
cartridges, explosive compositions and products for tactical and 
strategic applications. The Company also undertakes operation 
and maintenance services for solid propellant facilities at ISRO’s 
Sriharikota Centre.
Capabilities that support visibility
As of May 2026, the order book stood at Rs 15,690 million, equal 
to about 4.04 times FY26 revenue. Defence orders comprised 
around 95% of this order book, providing a stronger base for future 
execution. With six manufacturing facilities across Telangana, 
Madhya Pradesh and Maharashtra, a team of more than 850 people, 
and around 100 engineers and scientists, PEL is positioned as a 
specialised partner in India’s defence and space value chain.
During FY26, our revenue mix continued to shift towards Defence 
and Space, supported by a larger defence-led order pipeline.

Chairman’s Communication
Building with Responsibility and Readiness
The year’s financial numbers 
need to be read in this 
context. Revenue from 
operations stood at ₹3,883.4 
million compared with

This reduction was largely 
linked to stoppage of electric

Even with this revenue

demonstrate the resilience

through a balanced portfolio.
Dear Shareholders,
FY26 was a year of transition, consolidation 
and renewed preparedness for Premier 
Explosives Limited. We entered the year 
with the responsibility of strengthening our 
safety systems after the tragic incidents at 
our Peddakandukuru and Katepally facilities. 
Those events remain a sobering reminder 
that in our industry, operational excellence 
must begin with a disciplined safety culture. 
During the year, management continued to 
reinforce safety protocols, training practices, 
review mechanisms and accountability 
across our operating locations. This will 
remain an uncompromising priority.
At the same time, the Company continued 
to serve critical national programmes in 
defence and space. Premier Explosives 
operates in a specialised segment where 
credibility is earned over years through 
technical consistency, product reliability and 
the ability to meet demanding customer 
requirements. Our role as a manufacturer 
of high-energy materials, solid propellants, 
pyrotechnic devices, countermeasures for 
defence and aerospace, places us at an 
important intersection of industrial activity 
and national capability creation.
The year’s financial numbers need to be read 
in this context. Revenue from operations 
stood at ₹3,883.4 million compared with 
₹4,174.5 million in FY25. This reduction 
was largely linked to stoppage of electric 
detonator production. Even with this 
revenue moderation, the Company delivered 
a stronger bottom-line performance. EBIT

Our manufacturing platform remains 
central to this positioning. We operate six 
manufacturing facilities across Telangana, 
Madhya Pradesh and Maharashtra. The 
facilities at Peddakandukur and Katepally 
manufacture explosive accessories, 
defence explosives, solid propellants, 
PETN, ammonium perchlorate, HMX/
RDX, rocket motors, ammunition, mines, 
warheads and related products. Our bulk 
explosives facilities at Singrauli, Chandrapur, 
Godavarikhani and Manuguru continue to 
serve mining and infrastructure customers. 
Together, these locations give the Company 
a broad yet focused production base.
Our people continue to be our strength. 
Premier Explosives has a workforce of more 
than 850 experienced employees, including 
about 100 engineers and scientists. Around 
550 people have been trained in propellant 
and pyrotechnic products. In a field where 
knowledge, discipline and precision are 
indispensable, this talent base provides 
continuity and technical depth. I would 
like to acknowledge the contribution of 
every employee who has worked through 
a demanding operating environment with 
commitment and focus.
The external environment remains 
supportive for specialised Indian defence 
manufacturers. Government emphasis on 
Make in India, import substitution, domestic 
procurement and defence exports is creating 
a larger role for private sector participation. 
The national defence production base is 
expanding, and opportunities are emerging 
in missile systems, countermeasures, 
ammunition, mines, warheads, drones, 
loitering munitions and related high-
energy products. Premier Explosives has the 
experience and licence base to participate in 
this long-term opportunity with prudence.
The Company’s research and development 
orientation remains unchanged. Our DSIR-
certified and NABL-accredited laboratories, 
along with academic collaborations 
with Gulbarga University, IIT Madras and 
BITS Pilani, support work in high-energy 
materials. The development of combustion-
stable propellants and specialised 
compositions for missile programmes 
reflects our commitment to indigenous 
capability. We will continue to build this 
platform with a clear focus on reliability, 
safety and relevance.

is to strengthen Premier Explosives as 
a responsible, technically capable and 
nationally relevant enterprise. We will 
continue to pursue this objective with 
humility, discipline and a long-term view.

Dear Shareholders,
I am pleased to present the FY26 review 
of Premier Explosives Limited. The year 
required us to balance three priorities: 
strengthening operational discipline, 
executing ongoing customer commitments, 
and preparing the organisation for a larger 
defence-led opportunity pipeline. Our 
performance reflects this balance.
Safety and accountability remained at 
the centre of our management agenda. 
Following the incidents reported in the 
previous period, we continued to examine 
our systems, processes and training 
practices with greater intensity. In high-
energy materials, safety cannot be treated 
as a compliance activity alone; it must 
be embedded into decision-making, 
supervision, housekeeping, maintenance, 
handling and culture. We have therefore 
continued to strengthen reviews, protocols 
and employee awareness across our 
facilities.
The year’s financial numbers need to be read 
in this context. Revenue from operations 
stood at ₹3,883.4 million compared with 
₹4,174.5 million in FY25. This reduction 
was largely linked to stoppage of electric 
detonator production. Even with this 
revenue moderation, the Company delivered 
a stronger bottom-line performance. EBIT 
increased to ₹692.8 million and Profit After 
Tax rose to ₹458.3 million. Cash profit also 
improved to ₹575.1 million. These outcomes 
demonstrate the resilience of our operating

model and our ability to create value 
through a balanced portfolio.
The balance sheet continued to support the 
Company’s strategic direction. Total assets 
stood at ₹4,856.1 million as of March 2026. 
Other equity increased to ₹2,782.8 million, 
while current liabilities reduced to ₹1,609.4

2025. Capital work-in-progress increased to 
₹263.9 million, reflecting ongoing capacity 
and capability-related investments. These 
numbers indicate a more measured and 
better-capitalised platform for future 
execution.
The most important indicator of future 
visibility is our order book. As of May 
2026, Premier Explosives had its highest-
ever order book of ₹15,690 million. This is 
approximately 4.04 times FY26 revenue. 
Around 95% of the order book is from the 
Defence segment, with the balance from 
explosives and services. This order book is a 
clear signal of customer confidence in our 
technical ability, licence base and delivery 
record. It also gives us a stronger foundation 
for capacity planning, procurement, staffing 
and execution prioritisation.
Our Defence and Space portfolio continues 
to be the main driver of the Company’s 
strategic direction. We are engaged across 
propellants, pyros, explosive compositions, 
countermeasures and O&M services. Our 
product range includes pyrogen igniters, 
case-bonded propellants, free-standing 
propellant grains, fuel-rich propellant 
grains, gas generators, rocket motors, pyro 
cartridges, pyro actuators, smoke and flash 
generators, IR generators, squibs, PETN, 
HMX/RDX compounds, CL-20, Chaffs and 
Flares. We are also the only qualified Indian 
company for countermeasures and have 
capability in the export of fully assembled 
rocket motors.
Our contribution to India’s missile 
programmes remains a matter of national 
responsibility. We support platforms such 
as Akash, MRSAM, LRSAM, Astra, Agni 
and BrahMos through booster grains, 
sustainer grains, solid propellants, pyrogen 
igniters and rocket motor production 
and integration. These are demanding 
programmes, and our relevance depends on 
precision, documentation, quality assurance 
and timely execution.
Commercial Explosives remains an 
important business line. Bulk explosives, 
cast boosters, detonators and detonating 
fuse serve mining, infrastructure and cement 
customers in India and overseas markets. 
Although segment revenue reduced to ₹729 
million in FY26, the business continues to 
provide a long-standing industrial base, 
customer relationships and manufacturing 
continuity. We will pursue this segment 
selectively while maintaining focus on 
margins, working capital and export 
opportunities.
Research and development will remain 
a differentiator. Our laboratories are 
DSIR-certified and NABL-accredited, and 
we continue to benefit from academic 
collaborations. The Company’s work 
in combustion-stable propellants and 
specialised high-energy materials supports 
both current programmes and future 
product expansion. We are also focused on 
enhancing our product range for mines, 
ammunition, bombs, warheads, missile 
integration and related defence systems.
Looking ahead, our priorities are clear. We 
will execute the order book with discipline, 
strengthen safety and quality systems, 
improve capacity utilisation, enhance 
production capabilities in high explosive 
raw materials, expand defence exports, 
and participate in missile integration and 
strategic partner opportunities wherever 
appropriate. We will do this without diluting 
our focus on governance and operational 
responsibility.
I thank our employees for their resilience 
and expertise, our customers for their 
continued trust, our Board for its guidance, 
and our shareholders for their confidence. 
Premier Explosives is moving into the next 
phase with a stronger order book, deeper 
defence relevance and a sharper execution 
agenda.

T V Chowdary

Milestones in Indigenous Capability Creation
Foundation and early 
development
Premier Explosives Limited was 
incorporated in 1980 by Dr. A. N. 
Gupta, an alumnus of the Indian 
School of Mines, Dhanbad. The 
Company began with commercial 
explosives and detonators based on 
indigenous technology, establishing 
a foundation in energetic materials. 
It became a public limited company 
in 1987, broadening its institutional 
base for future expansion.
Research, certification and 
collaboration
The Company built research 
partnerships with Gulbarga 
University, IIT Madras and BITS 
Pilani. Its laboratories received DSIR 
certification and NABL accreditation, 
supporting product development in 
high-energy materials, propellants 
and specialised compositions.
Expanding industrial 
capability
During the 1980s and 1990s, PEL 
added detonating fuse, detonator 
and site-mixed slurry facilities, while 
also strengthening quality and 
research systems. Its R&D centre 
received recognition, and the 
Company progressively widened 
its commercial explosives and 
accessories portfolio for mining and 
infrastructure customers.
Innovation in safer 
initiation systems
PEL became the first company 
globally to commercialise NHN-
based detonators, an alternative 
to conventional primary charges. 
This achievement reflected the 
Company’s ability to combine safety, 
environmental consideration and 
commercial-scale manufacturing.
Entering strategic 
materials
A decisive shift took place in 2003, 
when PEL entered the development 
of solid propellants. This marked the 
Company’s movement from industrial 
explosives into defence-aligned high-
energy materials. It later became 
a sole private sector supplier of 
propellants for missile systems such 
as Akash, Astra, MRSAM and LRSAM.
Building FY26 readiness
By May 2026, PEL had achieved its 
highest-ever order book of  
₹ 15,690 million, with around 95% 
from Defence. This milestone reflects 
the Company’s evolution into a 
specialised partner for national 
defence and space programmes.
The Company’s milestones show a 
steady movement from indigenous 
industrial explosives to strategic 
systems. FY26 adds a new marker 
to this journey: a defence-led order 
book that is larger than any previous 
level disclosed by the Company.
6
Premier Explosives Limited 46th Annual Report 2025-26
[page 10]
Deep participation in 
national programmes
PEL supplies and supports critical 
products for customers such as 
DRDO, ISRO, BDL and other defence 
and space entities. Its contributions 
to Akash, Astra, MRSAM, LRSAM, 
Agni and BrahMos programmes 
demonstrate its relevance in missile 
propulsion, pyrotechnics and rocket 
motor applications.
Position in 
countermeasures and 
rocket motors
The Company is the only qualified 
Indian company for countermeasures 
and also specialises in the export of 
fully assembled rocket motors. These 
capabilities place it in segments 
where technical qualification, safety 
systems and customer trust are 
difficult to replicate.
Alignment with self-reliance priorities
The Company’s product development, manufacturing licences and defence relationships are aligned with India’s Make in 
India and import-substitution agenda, creating long-term relevance across strategic applications.
These differentiators are mutually reinforcing. Technical qualification supports order visibility; order visibility justifies 
capacity investment; and capacity investment, when supported by safety and quality systems, strengthens customer 
confidence. This cycle is central to PEL ’s FY26 positioning.
High visibility order book
As of May 2026, the Company’s order 
book stood at ₹ 15,690 million, equal 
to about 4.04 times FY26 revenue. 
Defence accounted for approximately 
95% of this order book, providing 
a stronger execution base and 
improving medium-term visibility.
Research-led 
development platform
With DSIR-certified and NABL-
accredited laboratories, and 
collaborations with Gulbarga 
University, IIT Madras and BITS 
Pilani, PEL continues to invest in 
high-energy materials. Its work on 
combustion-stable propellants and 
specialised compositions supports 
future readiness.
Integrated energetic-
material capability
PEL ’s capabilities extend across solid 
propellants, pyrogen igniters, pyro 
devices, explosive compositions, 
countermeasures, detonators, 
detonating fuse, cast boosters 
and bulk explosives. This breadth 
enables the Company to address 
both commercial and mission-critical 
requirements.
Manufacturing base and 
skilled people
PEL operates six manufacturing 
facilities across Telangana, Madhya 
Pradesh and Maharashtra. Its team of 
more than 850 employees, including 
100 engineers and scientists, and 
around 550 personnel trained in 
propellant and pyrotechnic products.
What Sets Premier Explosives Apart
Premier Explosives Limited brings together industrial experience, defence relevance and high-entry-
barrier manufacturing capabilities. The following strengths define its strategic position.

Business Portfolio Review
Premier Explosives Limited operates across Defence and Aerospace, and Industrial Explosives. In FY26, 
Defence and Space services contributed ₹ 3,154 million, representing 81% of revenue, while Industrial 
Explosives contributed ₹ 729 million. The business mix continues to specialised high-energy materials, 
countermeasures, propellants and strategic systems, supported by a long-standing industrial explosives 
base and recurring relationships with defence, space, mining and infrastructure customers.
Our FY26 business profile was led by Defence and Space, supported by improved profitability and a larger forward order pipeline, while 
Commercial Explosives remained an established operating vertical.
Commercial Explosives Defence and Space services
19%
81%
8
Premier Explosives Limited 46th Annual Report 2025-26
[page 12]
Premier Explosives Limited is positioned as a specialised manufacturer and service provider in high-
energy materials for defence and space applications. The segment generated ₹ 3,154 million in FY26 and 
accounted for 81% of the Company’s annual revenue. In Q4FY26, segment revenue increased 43.1% year-
on-year to ₹ 677 million, reflecting stronger execution during the quarter.
Strategic Defence and Space Capabilities
The Company manufactures products across 
propellants, pyros, explosive compositions 
and countermeasure systems. Its offerings 
include pyrogen igniters, case-bonded 
propellants, free-standing propellant grains, 
fuel-rich propellant grains, gas generators, 
rocket motors of 40 mm to 2000 mm 
diameter, pyro cartridges, pyro actuators, 
smoke and flash generators, IR generators, 
specialised squibs, PETN, HMX/RDX 
compounds, CL-20, Chaffs and Flares.
PEL supports defence and space public 
sector customers and strategic agencies 
through both products and services. It has 
contributed to major missile programmes, 
including Akash, MRSAM, LRSAM, Astra, 
Agni and BrahMos. Its role covers solid 
propellants, booster grains, sustainer grains, 
pyro initiators, pyrogen igniters and rocket 
motors.
The Company also undertakes O&M 
services for solid propellant plants at ISRO’s 
Sriharikota Centre. These assignments 
reflect the trust placed in PEL for sensitive, 
process-intensive operations.
The FY26 order book strengthens the 
segment’s outlook. Of the ₹ 15,690 million 
order book as of May 2026, about 95% is 
from Defence. This provides a stronger 
platform for future revenue conversion, 
capacity planning and participation in 
India’s defence self-reliance programmes.
The segment also benefits from the 
national emphasis on indigenisation and 
domestic sourcing. For PEL, this creates 
opportunities not only in existing missile 
and countermeasure products, but also in 
adjacent areas such as ammunition, mines, 
warheads, drones, loitering munitions and 
missile refurbishment. The Company’s task 
is to convert these opportunities through 
qualification, careful investment and 
consistent delivery.

Supporting India’s Launch Ecosystem
high-energy materials, quality procedures 
and sensitive manufacturing environments 
enables it to support programmes where 
safety and repeatability are essential.
The Defence and Space services segment 
reported revenue of ₹ 3,154 million in FY26. 
While annual revenue was lower than FY25 
due to execution timing in certain high-
value orders, the segment continued to 
account for the major share of Company 
revenue. The segment’s future visibility 
improved significantly with the overall 
order book reaching ₹ 15,690 million as 
of May 2026, with defence-related orders 
comprising the dominant share.
PEL remains a contributor to India’s space 
programme through energetic-material 
products and operations support linked 
to launch vehicle requirements. Its role is 
particularly important in solid propellant 
handling, mixing, casting, integration and 
related services at ISRO’s Sriharikota Centre 
under the O&M framework.
Key space-linked products and services 
include PSOM-XL solid propellant strap-
on motors for satellite launch vehicles, 
solid propellant processing support, and 
explosive and pyrotechnic devices used in 
launch vehicle and satellite deployment 
systems. The Company’s experience in 
Strategically, the space-linked portfolio 
reinforces PEL ’s position as a trusted 
partner in national programmes, while 
creating adjacency benefits for propellant, 
pyrotechnic and missile-related capabilities.
O&M services extend the Company’s 
technical strengths into process-intensive 
assignments. PEL manages solid propellant-
facilities at ISRO’s Sriharikota Centre. These 
services complement its product portfolio 
and reinforce customer confidence in its 
operating discipline.

Industrial Explosives
PEL ’s commercial explosives products are 
used in coal, iron ore, limestone, road, dam, 
tunnel and civil engineering applications. 
The customer base includes Coal India, 
Singareni Collieries, cement manufacturers, 
construction contractors and mining 
customers. Export markets include Israel, 
Greece, Jordan, Turkey, Nepal, Thailand, the 
Philippines, Indonesia, Djibouti Srilanka and 
Egypt.
Commercial Explosives represents the 
business from which PEL began its journey. 
The segment serves mining, infrastructure 
and industrial customers through bulk 
explosives, cast boosters, detonators and 
detonating fuse. In FY26, Commercial 
Explosives revenue stood at ₹ 729 million 
compared with ₹ 801 million in FY25. 
Q4FY26 revenue was ₹ 215 million, reflecting 
8.6% sequential growth over Q3FY26.
The Company will continue to serve 
commercial explosives customers selectively 
while prioritising safety, working capital 
discipline, export opportunities and 
alignment with higher-value defence and 
space work.
The segment also supports the Company’s 
operational learning in explosives handling, 
logistics, customer service and regulatory 
compliance, which remain relevant across 
the broader high-energy materials business.

for high-explosive raw materials such as

Management Discussion and Analysis 
1.  Global economic overview
 The global economy remained resilient during FY26, but the 
operating environment continued to be shaped by geopolitical 
tensions, trade fragmentation, supply-chain reconfiguration 
and financial-market volatility. These conditions affected the 
availability, pricing and lead times of specialised chemicals, 
metals and energetic-material inputs. For manufacturers of high-
energy materials, procurement discipline, inventory planning 
and qualification of alternate sources remained important 
operating priorities.
 The Economic Survey 2025-26 characterised the external 
environment as fragile, with elevated risks from geopolitical 
tensions, trade fragmentation and financial vulnerabilities. 
Against this backdrop, defence expenditure and the localisation 
of strategic supply chains remained policy priorities across 
several countries. For Indian defence manufacturers, the 
resulting environment created export opportunities, but also 
increased competition, compliance requirements and exposure 
to raw-material price movements.
2.  Indian economic overview
 India sustained a strong growth trajectory in FY26. Under the 
revised GDP series with base year 2022-23, real GDP growth for 
FY26 was estimated at 7.6%, compared with 7.1% in FY25. Growth 
was supported by consumption, investment, manufacturing 
and services, while public capital expenditure continued to 
support infrastructure creation. The broader macroeconomic 
environment remained favourable for domestic manufacturing 
and strategic-sector investment.
 For Premier Explosives Limited, the relevance of the domestic 
economic environment arises through three channels: public 
expenditure on defence and space; mining and infrastructure 
activity that supports demand for commercial explosives; and 
the development of domestic manufacturing ecosystems that 
improve access to specialised suppliers, testing infrastructure 
and skilled manpower.
3.  Industry overview
3.1 Indian defence manufacturing
 FY26 marked another year of expansion for India’s defence 
industrial base. Indigenous defence production reached a 
record Rs 1.78 lakh crore, an increase of 15.6% over FY25. The 
private sector contributed 24% of total production, up from 
22% in the preceding year, indicating a widening role for private 
manufacturers in strategic supply chains.
 Defence exports reached a record Rs 38,424 crore in FY26, 
increasing 62.66% over FY25. Indian defence products were 
exported to more than 80 countries, while the exporter 
base expanded to 145 entities. This growth reflects stronger 
international acceptance of Indian systems, sub-systems 
and components and creates a wider addressable market 
for qualified manufacturers of propellants, rocket motors, 
pyrotechnic devices, countermeasures and energetic materials.
 The Ministry of Defence fully utilised its revised capital outlay 
of Rs 1.86 lakh crore for FY26. Continued capital deployment, 
domestic procurement preferences, Positive Indigenisation 
Lists and simplified export procedures supported order visibility 
across the sector. These developments are relevant to PEL 
because its product portfolio participates in missile propulsion, 
countermeasures, high explosives, pyrotechnics and related 
strategic applications.
3.2 Missiles, air-defence systems and countermeasures
 The operating environment remained supportive for missile 
and air-defence programmes. India’s emphasis on indigenous 
tactical and strategic systems, replenishment requirements and 
platform modernisation sustained demand for solid propellants, 
pyrogen igniters, rocket motors, explosive compositions and 
countermeasure products. PEL ’s established participation in 
Akash, MRSAM, LRSAM, Astra, Agni and BrahMos programmes 
provides a relevant capability base; however, revenue conversion 
remains dependent on customer schedules, qualification cycles, 
inspection and delivery milestones.
3.3 Space sector
 India’s space programme continued to broaden through 
launch-vehicle missions, satellite deployment and increasing 
private-sector participation. ISRO’s FY26 programme sustained 
the requirement for reliable launch-vehicle subsystems and 
specialised operations. PEL ’s role includes O&M services for 
solid propellant facilities at Sriharikota and products such as 
PSOM-XL solid-propellant strap-on motors and pyrotechnic 
devices. The opportunity remains specialised and qualification-
intensive, with reliability and process discipline being central to 
participation.
3.4 Commercial explosives
 Demand for commercial explosives is linked to coal and mineral 
production, cement, quarrying and infrastructure activity. 
The segment remained competitive and sensitive to tender 
pricing, raw-material costs, customer mine plans and regulatory 
requirements. During FY26, the stoppage of electric detonator 
production affected the Company’s commercial explosives 
business. PEL continued to serve mining, infrastructure and 
cement customers through bulk explosives, cast boosters, 
detonating fuse and other permitted products, while 
maintaining an export presence in selected markets.
4.  Company overview and business model
 Premier Explosives Limited manufactures high-energy materials 
for defence, space, mining and infrastructure applications. The 
Company also undertakes O&M services for solid propellant 
plants at ISRO’s Sriharikota Centre. Its operations combine 
long-standing industrial-explosives experience with specialised 
defence capabilities in propellants, pyrotechnics, explosive 
compositions, countermeasures and rocket motors.
 PEL operates six manufacturing facilities across Telangana, 
Madhya Pradesh and Maharashtra. The Peddakandukur and 
Statutory Reports
21
46th Annual Report 2025-26 Premier Explosives Limited
[page 25]
Katepally facilities support accessories, defence explosives, 
propellants and specialised energetic materials, while facilities 
at Singrauli, Chandrapur, Godavarikhani and Manuguru serve 
bulk-explosives customers. The Company has more than 850 
employees, including around 100 engineers and scientists, 
and approximately 550 personnel trained in propellant and 
pyrotechnic products.
5.  Business segment review
5.1 Defence & Space services
 Defence & Space services remained the Company’s principal 
revenue contributor. Segment revenue was Rs 3,154 million 
in FY26 compared with Rs 3,374 million in FY25, a decline of 
6.5%. The segment accounted for 81% of FY26 revenue from 
operations. The year-on-year movement reflected execution 
timing and the larger volume of high-value Chaffs and Flares 
orders executed in FY25. Q4FY26 showed improved momentum, 
with segment revenue of Rs 677 million, 43.1% higher year-on-
year.
 The segment covers composite propellants, pyrotechnic devices, 
explosive compositions, countermeasures and O&M services. 
Products include pyrogen igniters, case-bonded propellants, 
free-standing and fuel-rich propellant grains, gas generators, 
rocket motors, pyro cartridges and actuators, smoke and flash 
generators, IR generators, specialised squibs, PETN, HMX/RDX 
compounds, CL-20, Chaffs and Flares. The Company is the only 
qualified Indian company for countermeasures and also has 
capability in the export of fully assembled rocket motors.
5.2 Commercial Explosives
 Commercial Explosives revenue was Rs 729 million in FY26 
compared with Rs 801 million in FY25, a decline of 9.0%. The 
segment contributed 19% of annual revenue. The decline was 
influenced by the stoppage of electric detonator production 
and lower segment execution. PEL continued to supply bulk 
explosives, cast boosters and detonating fuse to mining, 
infrastructure and cement customers, while pursuing permitted 
export opportunities.
5.3 Operations & Maintenance services
 The Company continued to undertake process-intensive O&M 
assignments for solid propellant facilities at ISRO’s Sriharikota 
Centre. This contract draw on PEL ’s experience in handling, 
mixing, casting and integration of solid propellant systems 
and represent recurring participation in India’s strategic 
programmes.
6. Order book and revenue visibility
 As of May 2026, PEL ’s order book stood at Rs 15,690 million, the 
highest disclosed by the Company and approximately 4.04 times 
FY26 revenue from operations. Defence accounted for about 
95% of the order book, Explosives for about 2% and Services 
for about 3%. The order book provides medium-term execution 
visibility, but its conversion will depend on customer delivery 
schedules, inspections, availability of inputs, qualification 
requirements and the Company’s capacity ramp-up.
7. Financial performance review
 The following discussion is based on the standalone FY26 results. 
Revenue from operations was Rs 3,883.4 million, 7.0% lower 
than Rs 4,174.5 million in FY25. Operating EBITDA was Rs 388.2 
million compared with Rs 580.0 million, and the EBITDA margin 
was 10.0% compared with 13.9%. The reduction reflected the 
lower revenue base and elevated raw-material prices during the 
year.
 Other income increased to Rs 421.4 million from Rs 21.2 million. 
Consequently, EBIT increased by 42.4% to Rs 692.8 million 
and the EBIT margin rose to 17.8% from 11.7%. Finance cost 
declined to Rs 36.5 million from Rs 111.0 million. Profit before 
tax increased by 74.8% to Rs 656.3 million. After an exceptional 
ex-gratia item of Rs 52.0 million and tax expense of Rs 146.1 
million, profit after tax was Rs 458.3 million, 60.5% higher than 
FY25. Cash profit increased by 43.7% to Rs 575.1 million, while 
earnings per share was Rs 8.5 compared with Rs 5.3.

7.1  Cost structure
 Cost of raw materials was Rs 2,238.6 million compared with Rs 
2,388.9 million. Employee benefit expense increased to Rs 689.1 
million from Rs 619.3 million, reflecting the requirements of a 
skilled and specialised workforce. Other expenses were Rs 567.5 
million compared with Rs 586.4 million. Raw-material volatility 
remained an important factor affecting operating margins.
7.2 Balance sheet and liquidity
 Total assets stood at Rs 4,856.1 million as of March 31, 2026 
compared with Rs 5,305.5 million a year earlier. Other equity 
increased to Rs 2,782.8 million from Rs 2,350 million. Current 
liabilities reduced to Rs 1,609.4 million from Rs 2,531.5 million, 
while current assets were Rs 2,581.2 million compared with  
Rs 3,192.2 million. The current ratio, computed from the 
disclosed balance-sheet figures, improved to approximately 
1.60 from 1.26. Capital work-in-progress increased to Rs 263.9 
million from Rs 28.5 million, reflecting ongoing capacity and 
capability-related investments.

8.  Research, development and technology
 Research and development remains central to PEL ’s position 
in high-entry-barrier products. The Company’s laboratories 
are recognised by the Department of Scientific and Industrial 
Research and accredited by the National Accreditation Board 
for Testing and Calibration Laboratories. PEL collaborates with 
Gulbarga University, IIT Madras and BITS Pilani for research in 
high-energy materials.
 The Company’s development work includes combustion-stable 
solid propellants for missile applications, specialised explosive 
compositions and products linked to countermeasures, 
rocket motors, warheads, mines and ammunition. R&D 
programmes involve long development and qualification cycles; 
commercialisation therefore depends on successful testing, 
customer approval and production orders.
9. Opportunities and outlook
• Execution of the Rs 15,690 million order book, particularly 
the 95% Defence component.
• Higher domestic defence production, which reached 
Rs 1.78 lakh crore in FY26, and rising private-sector 
participation.
• Defence exports of Rs 38,424 crore in FY26 and wider 
acceptance of Indian defence products across more than 
80 countries.
• Expansion into mines, ammunition, bombs, warheads, 
drones, loitering munitions, missile refurbishment and 
missile integration.
• Capacity enhancement for high-explosive raw materials 
and specialised energetic-material products.
• Continued participation in ISRO and DRDO-linked O&M 
and propulsion programmes.
• Selective development of commercial-explosives exports 
and mining-sector relationships.
 The outlook is supported by a strong order pipeline and 
favourable policy direction. The Company’s near-term priority 
is disciplined order execution, supported by safety, quality, 
working-capital management and timely capacity creation. 
Revenue may remain uneven across quarters because of the 
milestone-based and inspection-intensive nature of defence 
contracts.
10. Risks and concerns
Risk area Nature of exposure Management focus
Safety and process risk High-energy materials require exacting controls in 
manufacturing, storage, movement and handling.
Process discipline, training, maintenance, audits, 
incident learning and emergency preparedness.
Execution and customer-
schedule risk
Defence orders are linked to inspections, trials, 
programme schedules and milestone approvals.
Detailed production planning, customer 
coordination, documentation and capacity readiness.
Raw-material risk Specialised chemicals and energetic-material inputs 
can face price and availability volatility.
Vendor diversification, inventory planning, alternate-
source qualification and contract management.
Regulatory and licensing 
risk
Operations are subject to defence, explosives, 
environmental, labour and export-control 
regulations.
Licence compliance, regulatory monitoring, audits 
and controlled documentation.
Concentration risk The order book is predominantly Defence and 
includes institutional customers.
Portfolio expansion, exports, commercial explosives 
and broader product participation.
Technology and 
qualification risk
New products require extended testing and 
customer qualification.
Stage-gated R&D, collaboration, testing 
infrastructure and prudent capital allocation.
Working-capital risk Milestone-based contracts can create timing 
differences between procurement, production and 
collection.
Cash-flow planning, receivables monitoring, 
inventory control and banking discipline.
People risk Operations depend on specialised engineers, 
scientists and trained production personnel.
Training, succession, retention and safety-led 
workforce engagement.
Cyber and information-
security risk
Strategic programmes require protection of 
technical and commercial information.
Access controls, system security, backups, monitoring 
and employee awareness.

11.  Internal control systems and their adequacy
 The Company has internal control systems commensurate with 
the nature and scale of its operations. The control framework 
covers financial reporting, authorisation, procurement, 
inventory, production, quality, fixed assets, statutory compliance 
and safeguarding of assets. Internal audit observations and 
corrective actions are reviewed by management and the Audit 
Committee. The statutory auditors evaluate internal financial 
controls with reference to the financial statements in accordance 
with applicable standards.
12. Human resources and industrial relations
 PEL ’s operations require specialised knowledge, process 
discipline and a strong safety culture. The Company has a 
workforce of more than 850 employees, including around 
100 engineers and scientists, and approximately 550 people 
trained in propellant and pyrotechnic products. During FY26, 
the management focus remained on safety training, technical 
capability, supervision, accountability and employee awareness. 
Industrial relations remained focused on continuity of 
operations, compliance and workforce well-being.
13. Environment, health and safety
 Environment, health and safety are fundamental to the 
Company’s licence to operate. PEL continued to strengthen 
protocols, training, review mechanisms, maintenance practices 
and accountability across its facilities. The Company’s priorities 
include prevention of incidents, safe handling of energetic 
materials, regulatory compliance, emergency preparedness, 
responsible waste management and continuous improvement 
in operating practices.

2. State of affairs 
 The performance of the company in terms of Revenues has 
decreased during the year 2025-26. Operating EBITDA before 
exceptional items is Rs. 3882.18 lakhs as compared to Rs. 5799.68 
lakhs and Profit before tax and exceptional item is Rs.6563.17 
lakhs as compared to Rs. 3754.24 during previous year. Profit 
after tax stands at Rs.4582.51 lakhs as compared to Rs. 2855.26 
lakhs for the year 2024-25. The Raw Material supplies and prices 
were stabilized during the year which will give positive impact in 
near future. 
 Operations 
 Production of detonators was 4.56 million pieces as against 
10.36 million pieces in previous year. The Company has 
executed several orders in defence during the year. Operations 
& maintenance contracts at Sriharikota has been satisfactory 
during the Financial Year 2025-26.
 The production of bulk explosives increased to 9592 tonnes 
from previous year’s 8557 tonnes. 
3. Capital expenditure 
 During the year the company incurred the capital expenditure of 
Rs.653.52 lakhs on fixed assets and Rs.96.84 lakhs on intangible 
assets.

 The Board of Directors of your company, at their Meeting held 
on May 29, 2026, has recommended payment of Rs. 0.50/- 
(Rupees Fifty paise only) (25%) per equity share, as final dividend 
for the financial year ended March 31, 2026. The payment of final 
dividend is subject to the approval of the shareholders at the 
ensuing Annual General Meeting (AGM) of the Company.
 The dividend on equity shares for the financial year 2025-26 
would aggregate to Rs. 268.81 Lakhs.
 In view of the changes made under the Income-tax Act, 2025, 
dividends paid or distributed by the Company shall be taxable in 
the hands of the Shareholders. The Company shall, accordingly, 
make the payment of the final dividend after deduction of tax at 
source.

7. Change in the nature of business, if any
 During the year, there was no change in the nature of business 
of the company. 
 Accident at Katepally Facility
 During the financial year, on April 29, 2025, a fire and explosion 
occurred in the propellant mixing building at our Katepally plant/
facility, leading to significant structural and equipment damage 
and, most regrettably, the loss of four lives. The operations at 
the plant were temporarily paused to facilitate restoration of 
structures and replacement of equipment/machinery.
 We extend our sincere condolences to the families of the 
deceased and have taken steps to support them. The safety 
and well-being of our employees remain our highest priority. 
Comprehensive internal investigations have been conducted, 
and we are reinforcing our safety protocols, training programs, 
and operational procedures to prevent recurrence of such 
incidents.
 All affected assets were appropriately insured, and the Company 
has initiated claims under its insurance policies.
8. Material changes and commitments after the reporting 
period
 There have been no such material changes and commitments, 
affecting the financial position of the Company which have 
occurred between the end of financial year to which the 
Financial Statements relates and the date of this Report except 
for the following:
 A definitive Share Purchase Agreement (“SPA”) was executed on 
July 09, 2026, by and among the Company, Mrs. Shonika Prasad 
and Mrs. Kailash Gupta, in their capacity as the authorised 
trustees of the AKS Family Trust,(“ Promoter”), and the Acquirers, 
Apollo Micro Systems Limited, pursuant to which the Acquirer 
agreed to acquire 22221735 equity shares of Face Value of Rs. 2/- 
each of Premier Explosives Limited, representing 41.33% of the 
paid up equity share capital, for an aggregate cash consideration 
of approximately Rs.1,550 Crore. 
 This transaction has triggered the provisions of Regulation 3(1) 
and 4 of the SEBI (Substantial Acquisition of Shares and Takeovers) 
Regulations, 2011, including subsequent amendments (“SEBI 
SAST Regulations”). Accordingly, the Acquirer has made public 
announcement (PA) on July 10, 2026, a Detailed Public Statement 
(DPS) was issued on July 16, 2026, followed by the filing of the 
Draft Letter of Offer (DLOF) with SEBI on July 23, 2026, for open 
offer to the Public Shareholders of the Company to acquire 
upto 1,39,77,911 equity shares, representing 26 % of the equity 
share capital of the Company, at an offer price of Rs. 698/- per 
share, aggregating to a total consideration of Rs. 975,65,81,878/-
, payable in cash in accordance with the applicable regulatory 
framework. The consummation of this transaction is subject to 
receipt of applicable governmental and statutory/regulatory 
approvals including approval from the Competition Commission 
of India, wherever applicable, along with the fulfilment of other 
conditions precedent under the SPA.
 The transaction combines complementary capabilities in 
defence systems and energetic materials, enabling operational 
efficiencies, enhanced R&D, and broader participation in 
defence and space-related programmes.
 This acquisition strengthens our nation’s defence manufacturing 
capability and deepens our sovereign expertise in energetic 
materials and space programmes. Together, we are better 
positioned to safeguard national security interests and support 
our armed forces with greater scale, resilience, and technological 
self-reliance.
9. Subsidiary companies, Jointly controlled entity and 
consolidated financial statements 
a) PELNEXT Defence Systems Private Limited, a 100% 
subsidiary company
 Incorporated on July 15, 2016 PELNEXT is expected to be 
operated as a special purpose vehicle in defence explosives 
business. The company incurred a net loss of Rs. 0.80 lakh 
during 2025-26 (Rs. 0.91 lakh during 2024-25). 
 As on 31st March, 2026, Premier Explosives Limited held 
10,000 Equity shares in PELNEXT representing 100% of 
equity share capital. 
b) Premier Wire Products Limited (PWPL), an 80% 
subsidiary company
 PWPL was engaged in manufacture of Galvanised Iron 
(GI) Wire catering to the requirements of detonator-
manufacturers, having its registered office at 
Secunderabad, Telangana. The Company’s manufacturing 
facilities are located at Ramajipet, Yadadri Bhuvanagiri 
District of Telangana. Due to lower demand for GI wire from 
detonator-manufacturers, the company has sold major 
property, plant and equipment, dismantled building, 
terminated all the employees and intimated closure of 
factory to various authorities during the year 2021-22.
 The company incurred a net profit before tax of Rs. 8.00 
lakhs during the year ended March 31, 2026 and the 
company has accumulated profit of Rs. 16.96 lakhs as at 
March 31, 2026 (Revenue of Rs.Nil lakhs and Net Profit of 
Rs.15.91 lakhs during previous year). 
 As on 31st March, 2026, Premier Explosives Limited held 
52,00,000 Equity shares in PWPL representing 80% of their 
equity share capital.

10. Future outlook 
 PEL is embarking on newer challenges & missions for the 
Defence Forces, after establishing itself as the frontrunner in 
manufacturing countermeasures like Chaffs & Flares for the Air 
Force, PEL is now undertaking various types of mines which 
are being developed like Jumping Mine by the name of Ulka, 
another mine by the name of Viraj. PEL has almost completed 
production of Canopy Severance System (CSS) for all fighter 
Planes of the Air Force.
 PEL has entered the field of Loitering Munitions and designing & 
developing explosive Pay Loads for UAVs and Drones. 
 The Company is working with complete proficient nimbleness 
& an inclination for precision to ensure that the right store is 
delivered at the right place and the right time to ensure that our 
defense forces live up to all challenges against our adversaries.

G. Company’s policy on appointment and remuneration 
of directors 
a) Criteria for appointment of directors 
 Director must have relevant experience in finance, 
law, management, sales, marketing, administration, 
research, corporate governance, technical operations 
or other disciplines related to company’s business.
 Director should possess the highest personal and 
professional ethics, integrity and values.
 Director must be willing to devote sufficient time and 
energy in carrying out their duties and responsibilities.
 Nomination and Remuneration Committee shall 
identify and ascertain the integrity, qualification, 
expertise and experience of the person for 
appointment as director and recommend to the 
Board his / her appointment or re-appointment.
 The committee has discretion to decide whether 
qualification, expertise and experience possessed 
by a person are sufficient or satisfactory for the 
concerned position.
 While appointing an independent director, 
Nomination and Remuneration Committee shall 
consider the ‘independence’ of the person also in 
addition to the above.
b) Policy on directors’ remuneration
i. Policy
 The Company shall remunerate its directors, 
key managerial personnel, senior management, 
other employees and workers appropriately to 
retain and motivate them as well as to attract 
new talent when required.
ii. Components of remuneration
 Remuneration package shall include fixed 
component for all employees and variable 
component to the extent desirable and 
practicable.
iii. Fixed remuneration 
 It shall be competitive and based on 
the individual’s education, experience, 
responsibilities, performance, industry 
benchmark in the area, etc.
 Fixed remuneration shall comprise of basic 
salary and other allowances like house rent 
allowance, conveyance allowance, etc. which 
are calculated as certain % of basic salary.
iv. Variable remuneration 
 It is paid to encourage the employees to achieve set targets and variable remuneration shall be determined on the 
following basis:
Category Nature Basis of variable remuneration
Whole time Directors Commission X% of Profit in a year during the contract period (% as 
recommended by Board and approved by Shareholders.
Management Team
(CFO, Company Secretary, 
President, Vice President, GM)
Profit sharing bonus X% of Profit divided among them in proportion of their basic 
salary (% as decided by Committee of Whole time Directors)
Officers (Below GM level) Profit sharing bonus X% of Profit divided among them in proportion of their basic 
salary.(Minimum period of services and other conditions for 
eligibility are decided by Committee of Whole time Directors)
Staff and Workers Production incentive Quantity of production, as per the Wage Agreement revised 
every 3 years at Peddakandukuru (Those who are engaged in 
production and allied activities are eligible.
v. Statutory benefits 
 Employee benefits like Contribution to Provident Fund, Gratuity, Bonus, Employees State Insurance, Workmen 
Compensation, etc. shall be provided to all eligible employees as per the respective Acts.

vii. Increments
 Increments are made taking into account the individual performance, inflation and company performance.
 Workers are given Variable Dearness Allowance as per Consumer Price Index semi-annually on 1st of April and 1st of 
October. 
 Wages of workers at Peddakandukuru are revised every 3 years as per the agreement between the management and 
unions.
 Increments of other employees are made effective 1st April every year, as approved by Committee of Whole time Directors 
upon recommendation of heads of departments.
 Mid-year increments are given in exceptional cases, as approved by the Managing Director, upon recommendation of 
concerned director and head of department.
viii. Remuneration to Independent and Non-Executive Directors
 Remuneration / Commission shall be in accordance with the statutory provisions of the Companies Act, 2013 and the 
rules made thereunder and the Listing Regulations, for the time being in force and shall be entitled to such sitting fee in 
respect of the Board and Committee meetings attended, at the rates approved by the Board and within the applicable 
provisions of the Companies Act, 2013.
ix. Service contracts, notice period and severance fees:
 Executive directors have entered into a service contracts with the company. The tenure of the contract is three/five years. 
Reappointment is done by the Board based on the recommendation of the Nomination and Remuneration Committee. 
Notice period is as mutually agreed between the director and the Board.
 None of the directors is eligible for severance pay.

29. Disclosure of internal financial control systems and their 
adequacy Rule 8(5)(viii) of the Companies (Accounts) Rules 
2014 
 In view of the significant increase in the scale, size and complexity 
of the Company’s operations in recent years, the Company has, 
with effect from July 2026, further strengthened its existing 
internal financial control framework to ensure that it remains 
adequate and commensurate with the size, scale, nature and 
complexity of its business. The Company has also introduced 
additional Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) and control 
measures to further enhance the effectiveness and adequacy of 
its internal financial controls.
 The internal financial controls are designed to provide 
reasonable assurance with regard to the reliability of financial 
reporting, safeguarding of its assets, prevention and detection 
of frauds and errors, accuracy and completeness of accounting 
records, and aid in the timely preparation of reliable financial 
information.

goals and objectives of the company. Your company has been practicing the principles of corporate governance over the years.

Rules made there under, SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015 (“Listing Regulations”) and other

1. Audit Committee
a. Brief description of Terms of reference
 Audit committee reviews the audit reports submitted by the Internal Auditors and Statutory Auditors, Financial results, the 
effectiveness of the Internal Audit process, Management Discussion and Analysis report, Related Party Transactions, etc. These terms 
of reference are in line with the regulatory requirements mandated by the Section 177 of the Companies Act, 2013 and Part C of 
Schedule II of Regulation 18(3) of SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements), Regulations 2015.
[page 43]
40
Premier Explosives Limited 46th Annual Report 2025-26
A. Role of Audit Committee includes
i. Oversight of the company’s financial reporting process and the disclosure of its financial information to ensure that the 
financial statement is correct, sufficient and credible.
ii. Review and recommending to the Board, of the appointment, re-appointment and, if required, the replacement or 
removal of statutory auditors and fixation of audit fees.
iii. Approval of payment to statutory auditors for any other services rendered by the statutory auditors.
iv. Reviewing with the management, the annual financial statements, before submission to the Board for approval, with 
particular reference to -
1. Matters required to be included in the Director’s Responsibility Statement to be included in the Board report in 
terms of clause (c) of sub section (3) of section 134 of the Companies Act, 2013.
2. Change, if any, in accounting policies and practices and reasons for the same.
3. Major accounting entries involving estimates based on the exercise of judgment by management.
4. Significant adjustments made in the financial statements arising out of audit findings.
5. Compliance with listing and other legal requirements relating to financial statements.
6. Disclosure of any related party transactions.
7. Modified opinion (s) in the draft audit report.
v. Reviewing, with the management, the quarterly financial statements before submission to the Board for approval.
vi. Reviewing with the management the statement of uses/application of funds raised through an issue (public issue, rights 
issue, preferential issue, etc), the statement of funds utilised for purposes other than those stated in the offer document/
prospectus/notice and the report submitted by the monitoring agency monitoring the utilisation of proceeds of a public 
or rights issue, and making appropriate recommendations to the board to take up steps in this matter.
vii. Review and monitor the auditor’s independence and performance and effectiveness of audit process.
viii. Approval of any subsequent modification of transactions of the Company with related parties.
ix. Scrutiny of inter-corporate loans and investments.
x. Valuation of undertakings or assets of the listed entity, wherever it is necessary.
xi. Evaluation of internal financial controls and risk management systems
xii. Reviewing with the Management, performance of statutory and internal auditors, and adequacy of the internal control 
systems.
xiii. Reviewing the adequacy of internal audit function, if any, including the structure of the internal audit department, staffing 
and seniority of the official heading the department, reporting structure coverage and frequency of internal audit
xiv. Discussion with internal auditors of any significant findings and follow up thereon.
xv. Reviewing the findings of any internal investigations by the internal auditors into matters where there is suspected fraud 
or irregularity or a failure of internal control systems of a material nature and reporting the matter to the Board
xvi. Discussion with statutory auditors before the audit commences, about the nature and scope of audit as well as post audit 
discussion to ascertain any area of concern.
xvii. To look into reasons for substantial defaults in the payments to the depositors, debenture holders, shareholders (in case 
of non-payment of declared dividends) and creditors
xviii. To review the functioning of the whistle blower mechanism
xix. Approval of appointment of Chief Financial Officer after assessing the qualifications, experience and background, etc., of 
the candidate.
xx. Carrying out any other function as is mentioned in the terms of reference of the Audit Committee.
xxi. To review the utilisation of loans and/or advances from/investment by the holding company in the subsidiary exceeding 
rupees 100 crore or 10% of the asset size of the subsidiary whichever is lower including existing loans / advances / 
investments.
xxii. Consider and comment on rationale, cost-benefits and impact of schemes involving merger, demerger, amalgamation 
etc., on the listed entity and its shareholders.
[page 44]

• For every appointment of an independent director, the Nomination and Remuneration Committee shall evaluate the balance 
of skills, knowledge and experience on the Board and on the basis of such evaluation, prepare a description of the role and 
capabilities required of an independent director. The person recommended to the Board for appointment as an independent 
director shall have the capabilities identified in such description. For the purpose of identifying suitable candidates, the 
Committee may:
a. Use the services of an external agencies, if required;
b. Consider candidates from a wide range of backgrounds, having due regard to diversity; and
c. Consider the time commitments of the candidates.

bonus. The compensation is determined based on the remuneration prevailing in the industry and the performance of the 
company. The remuneration package of the executive Directors is periodically reviewed and suitable revision is recommended 
to the Board by the Nomination and Remuneration Committee within the limits approved by the shareholders.

Commission is paid to Dr. Amarnath Gupta, Non-Executive Director, as recommended to the Board by the Nomination & 
Remuneration Committee and approved by shareholders and as per the limits prescribed under Section 197 of the Companies 
Act, 2013.

e. Criteria for performance evaluation
 Performance evaluation criteria for Independent Directors
 The performance evaluation criteria for independent directors are determined by the Nomination and Remuneration committee. 
Factors of evaluation include participation and contribution by a director, commitment, effective deployment of knowledge and 
expertise, integrity and maintenance of confidentiality and independence of behaviour and judgement.
 Executive Directors
 Performance of the Executive Directors is evaluated on broad criteria such as contribution and value addition to the Board and 
Committees thereof; contribution to the Company and management to achieve its plans, goals, corporate strategy and risk 
mitigation; level of participation in the Board and Committee meetings, etc. Director being evaluated does not participate in 
the evaluation process. The performance of Board as a whole is evaluated by the Independent Directors on the basis of its duties 
and responsibilities as per terms of reference. The Chairman’s performance is evaluated by Independent Directors on the above 
parameters after taking into account the views of Executive and Non-Executive Directors.
3. Meeting of Independent Directors
 A separate meeting of Independent Directors of the Company was held on 12th February, 2026. At the meeting, the Independent Directors 
reviewed the performance of the Non-Independent Directors and the Board as a whole; reviewed the performance of the Chairman of the 
Company, taking into account the views of the Executive and Non executive Directors and assessed the quality, quantity and timeliness 
of flow of information between the Company Management and the Board that is necessary for the Board to effectively and reasonably 
perform its duties.
 The Independent Directors expressed their satisfaction with the overall performance of the Directors and the Board as a whole.
4. Succession planning
 The Nomination and Remuneration Committee works with the Board on succession plan to ensure orderly succession in appointments 
to the Board and in the senior management. The Company strives to maintain an appropriate balance of skills and experience within the 
Board of Directors and the Organisation.

8. Risk Management Committee
 Brief Description of Terms of Reference
1. To formulate a detailed risk management policy which shall include:
a. A framework for identification of internal and external risks specifically faced by the listed entity, in particular including 
financial, operational, sectoral, sustainability (particularly, ESG related risks), information, cyber security risks or any other risk 
as may be determined by the Committee.
b. Measures for risk mitigation including systems and processes for internal control of identified risks.
c. Business continuity plan.
2. To ensure that appropriate methodology, processes and systems are in place to monitor and evaluate risks associated with the 
business of the Company;
3. To monitor and oversee implementation of the risk management policy, including evaluating the adequacy of risk management 
systems;
4. To periodically review the risk management policy, at least once in two years, including by considering the changing industry 
dynamics and evolving complexity;
5. To keep the board of directors informed about the nature and content of its discussions, recommendations and actions to be taken;
6. The appointment, removal and terms of remuneration of the Chief Risk Officer (if any) shall be subject to review by the Risk 
Management Committee.

1. Brief outline of the Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) Policy
 The Company’s CSR policy is in alignment with the guidelines provided by the Ministry of Corporate Affairs (MCA). The Board has formulated 
a CSR Policy with the main objective that “the Company shall undertake the CSR activities that help the surrounding communities possible 
in its means and meeting the regulatory requirements.
 Details of the policy can be seen at the company’s website: https://www.pelgel.com/codconcsr.html

2.  Sustainable Sourcing
a.  Does the entity have procedures in place for sustainable sourcing? (Y es/No) Yes. The Company has established procurement 
procedures that encourage sourcing from approved and compliant suppliers while considering quality, regulatory compliance, 
ethical business practices, and environmental and social aspects, wherever applicable. The Company continues to strengthen its 
sustainable sourcing practices in line with its business requirements and applicable regulations.
b.  If yes, what percentage of inputs were sourced sustainably? The Company maintains records of its procurement; however, the 
percentage of inputs sourced sustainably is not currently tracked separately. Wherever feasible, the Company procures materials 
from reputable and compliant suppliers who are expected to follow applicable environmental, quality, and ethical standards, and it 
is committed to further strengthening its sustainable sourcing practices.
[page 81]
78
Premier Explosives Limited 46th Annual Report 2025-26
3.  Describe the processes in place to reclaim products for reusing, recycling, and disposing at the end of life for
a.  Plastics ( Including Packaging ) : No plastic waste is generated from operations. Product packaging is not reclaimed, as products 
are supplied to institutional customers under statutory transport and handling regulations (refer Leadership Indicator 4).
b.  E-Waste: The E-waste will be disposed by sending to TGPCB authorized recyclers.
c.  Hazardous waste: Regarding hazardous waste management, we generate two types: Solid and Liquid. For the disposal of 
solid hazardous waste, Premier Explosives has an agreement with the Hyderabad Waste Management Project in Dundigal, 
MedchalMalkajgiri District. For liquid effluent, we have an agreement with the MANA Effluent Treatment Plant in Mallapur, Hyderabad
d.  Other waste : As a manufacturer of explosives, we are unable to send any explosive-contaminated material or residual explosives 
to third parties due to their inherent explosive nature. Therefore, such materials are disposed of within our factory premises at 
designated locations, in accordance with the Explosive Rules 2008.
4.  Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR)
 Whether Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR) is applicable to the entity’s activities (Y es / No). If yes, whether the waste 
collection plan is in line with the Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR) plan submitted to Pollution Control Boards? If not, 
steps taken to address the same.
 Extended Producer Responsibility is not applicable as the Company is not a producer, importer or brand owner of plastic packaging, 
electronics or batteries; e-waste generated as a consumer is channeled to TGPCB authorized recyclers.

3. Accessibility of workplaces
 Are the premises / offices of the entity accessible to differently abled employees and workers, as per the requirements of the 
Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016? If not, whether any steps are being taken by the entity in this regard.
 Yes, The corporate office (“Premier House”) and non-hazardous administrative blocks across plant locations are equipped with basic 
physical accessibility features, including ground-floor access, wheelchair ramps, and accessible restrooms., in alignment with the Rights 
of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016. However, the Company operates under the strict regulatory oversight of the Explosives Act, 1884, 
Explosives Rules, 2008, and PESO regulations. These statutory frameworks explicitly prohibit the employment and engagement of 
persons with physical or mental challenges in direct explosive handling, manufacturing, and high-consequence operational zones due to 
extreme safety risks and critical emergency egress requirements. Accordingly, accessibility and employment in direct process plant areas 
are restricted in compliance with these overarching statutory mandates.
4. Does the entity have an equal opportunity policy as per the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016? If so, provide a web-link 
to the policy.
 Yes, Policy can be accessed at https://www.pelgel.com/codconcsr.html

12.  Describe the measures taken by the entity to ensure a safe and healthy work-place.
 Personal Protective Equipment (PPE): Ensure employees have access to and use appropriate PPE for their tasks and work environment
 House keeping : Maintain a clean and organized work environment to minimize hazards like slips, trips and falls
 Ergonomics: Provide ergonomic equipment and adjust workstations to fit employees’ needs, promoting physical well-being.
 Regular Inspections and Audits: Conduct regular safety inspections and audits to ensure compliance with safety policies and procedures.
 Safety Committees: Establish safety committees with representatives from different departments to promote employee involvement in 
safety initiatives.
 Leadership Commitment: Demonstrate a strong commitment to safety from top management, setting the tone for the entire organization.
 Emergency Preparedness: Develop and practice emergency response plans, including evacuation procedures, first aid and fire safety

Principle 4. Businesses should respect the interests of and be responsive to all its stakeholders.
Essential Indicators:
1.  Describe the process for identification of key stakeholder groups of the entity.
 The entity identifies its key stakeholders through a structured assessment process that considers the degree of influence stakeholders 
have on the company’s operations as well as the extent to which they are impacted by the company’s activities. This involves mapping 
stakeholder categories such as customers (defence establishments, aerospace agencies, and commercial clients), regulators and 
government authorities, employees, suppliers, investors, and local communities. The process combines internal reviews, management 
deliberations, and periodic engagement mechanisms to ensure that the most relevant groups are prioritized for dialogue and collaboration. 
Stakeholder mapping is revisited regularly to reflect changes in business context, regulatory landscape, and societal expectations.

46th Annual Report 2025-26 Premier Explosives Limited
Leadership Indicators:
1.  Provide the processes for consultation between stakeholders and the Board on economic, environmental, and social topics or if 
consultation is delegated, how is feedback from such consultations provided to the Board.
 The Company has established a structured stakeholder engagement process to identify and address key economic, environmental, and 
social matters. Feedback from stakeholders—including investors, employees, customers, suppliers, local communities, and regulatory 
authorities—is gathered through various formal and informal engagement mechanisms such as meetings, grievance channels, surveys, 
statutory interactions, and periodic reviews. Responsibility for stakeholder consultation is delegated to the respective functional heads 
and senior management, who evaluate the issues raised and escalate significant matters to the Board or its Committees through 
periodic management reports, risk assessments, compliance updates, and sustainability reviews. The Board considers this feedback while 
overseeing the Company’s strategy, risk management, governance, sustainability initiatives, and long-term value creation, ensuring that 
stakeholder perspectives are appropriately integrated into key business decisions.
2.  Whether stakeholder consultation is used to support the identification and management of environmental, and social topics (Y es 
/ No). If so, details of instances as to how the inputs received from stakeholders on these topics were incorporated into policies 
and activities of the entity.
 Yes. Inputs received through stakeholder engagement are considered while framing and reviewing the Company’s policies and activities. 
Illustratively, concerns raised by local communities on safety, employment and environment are addressed through strengthened 
safety communication, local employment and CSR initiatives in healthcare, education and environmental sustainability; feedback from 
customers on quality and delivery is incorporated through the Quality Management System; and suggestions from employees and 
workers on safety are acted upon through safety committee and building safety meetings
3.  Provide details of instances of engagement with, and actions taken to, address the concerns of vulnerable/ marginalized 
stakeholder groups.
 The Company recognizes local communities residing around its two manufacturing plants as a vulnerable and marginalized stakeholder 
group and actively engages with them through regular community meetings, project-specific consultations, CSR outreach programs, and 
interactions with local representatives. These engagements provide a platform to understand community concerns related to employment 
opportunities, health and safety, environmental management, infrastructure development, and overall community well-being. Feedback 
received from these interactions is considered while planning and implementing the Company’s Corporate Social Responsibility 
(CSR) initiatives. Based on identified needs, the Company undertakes CSR programs focused on education, healthcare, sanitation, skill 
development, environmental sustainability, and community infrastructure. The effectiveness of these initiatives is periodically reviewed 
through ongoing community engagement, enabling the Company to address concerns promptly and strengthen its relationship with the 
communities surrounding its manufacturing facilities.

6.  Describe the internal mechanisms in place to redress grievances related to human rights issues
 Yes, Employees are encouraged to promptly report any grievances to their immediate supervisor for resolution. In instances where the 
grievance pertains to the immediate supervisor, employees may directly approach the next-level manager. Still, if the employee is not 
satisfied with the resolution provided by the immediate supervisor/next level manager, they have the right to escalate the matter to the 
HR Department. This grievance redressal process ensures fairness, transparency, and accountability in addressing human rights-related 
concerns within the organization.

9.  Mechanisms to prevent adverse consequences to the complainant in discrimination and harassment cases.
 The Company has established multiple mechanisms to protect complainants against retaliation and ensure fair redressal of discrimination 
and harassment-related concerns. An Internal Committee (IC) has been constituted in accordance with the Sexual Harassment of Women 
at Workplace (Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal) Act, 2013 (POSH Act) to receive and investigate complaints of sexual harassment. 
The Company maintains strict confidentiality throughout the inquiry process and provides safeguards against retaliation or victimization 
of complainants, witnesses, and other participants, in accordance with the provisions of the POSH Act. In addition, the Company has 
implemented a Vigil Mechanism/Whistleblower Policy that enables employees, directors, and other stakeholders to report concerns 
relating to unethical conduct, discrimination, harassment, or other improper practices in good faith. Complaints may be submitted to 
designated reporting authorities, including supervisors or the Audit Committee, with an option for anonymous reporting. The Policy 
mandates confidentiality of the whistleblower’s identity, protection against reprisal or victimization, and investigation of complaints by 
the Audit Committee within defined timelines, with significant matters reported to the Board. Employees also have access to statutory 
grievance redressal mechanisms under applicable labour laws, including approaching the Labour Commissioner under the Industrial 
Disputes Act, wherever applicable. These mechanisms collectively ensure that complainants can raise concerns without fear of adverse 
consequences while promoting a transparent, ethical, and respectful workplace.

10.  Briefly describe the details of waste management practices adopted in your establishments. Describe the strategy adopted by 
your company to reduce usage of hazardous and toxic chemicals in your products and processes and the practices adopted to 
manage such wastes.
 Premier explosives have obtained Consent for Operation (CFO) for both our Peddakandukuru and Katepally units
 Peddakandukuru: CFO No. TSPCB/ZO/RCP/NLG/58/CFO/2021-1035, dated October 11, 2021, valid until October 31, 2026.
 Katepally: CFO No. TGPCB/ZO/RCP/NLG/1069/CFO/2025-250525746734, dated March 1, 2025, valid until January 31, 2030.
 Generally, the manufacturing process involves the assembly of hardware and the mixing of materials into a homogenous product, which 
typically does not generate any waste. Any residual explosives left over after the process are disposed of by burning with the necessary 
safety precautions at designated locations within our factory premises.
 Liquid effluent generated during the Nitration process is collected, neutralized, and then either sent to a Common Effluent Treatment 
Facility (in the case of Peddakandukuru) or treated in a forced evaporation plant, with the condensed water collected and reused (in the 
case of Katepally). All raw materials used in our processes comply with customer requirements or are approved by the Petroleum and 
Explosive Safety Organization (PESO).
 Regarding hazardous waste management, we generate two types: solid and liquid, these wastes are treated at the factory and transported 
to Telangana pollution control board authorised waste management facilities for further treatment and disposal as mentioned in the 
Consent for operation order.
 For the disposal of solid hazardous waste, Premier Explosives has an agreement with the Hyderabad Waste Management Project in 
Dundigal, Medchal-Malkajgiri district. For liquid effluent, we have an agreement with the MANA Effluent Treatment Plant in Mallapur, 
Hyderabad.
 As a manufacturer of explosives, we are unable to send any explosive-contaminated material or residual explosives to third parties 
due to their inherent explosive nature. Therefore, such materials are disposed of within our factory premises at designated locations, in 
accordance with the Explosive Rules 2008.

3.  Describe the mechanisms to receive and redress grievances of the community
 The Company has established accessible channels for communities to raise their concerns, primarily through dedicated email and 
phone contacts. All grievances received are formally recorded, acknowledged, and directed to the concerned team for examination and 
resolution. The process is designed to ensure timely responses, with unresolved matters escalated to higher levels of management where 
necessary. By maintaining these transparent and responsive mechanisms, the Company is able to address issues effectively and sustain 
constructive relations with the surrounding communities.
